# APPENDIX E — CANONICAL FIGURES REGISTER

**Where any Part disagrees with this table, this table governs.** Every figure carries its basis and date. Compiled 10 August 2026.

## E.1 Theory and taxonomy

| Item | Canonical value |
|---|---|
| Cook's property-rights problems | **Five**, not four — free-rider, horizon, portfolio, control, influence costs (Cook, *AJAE* 77(5), 1995, pp. 1153–1159) |
| Cook's lifecycle phases | Five — justification, design, growth/heterogeneity, introspection, choice |
| Failure modes used in this study | Four — demutualisation, absorption, degeneration, insolvency |
| Capture types (Part III) | Seven — managerial, board/insider, factional, staff, regional, external, political |

## E.2 Germany

| Item | Canonical value |
|---|---|
| Genossenschaftsgesetz | 1889, in force as amended (most recently 23 October 2024) |
| Delegate-assembly threshold | **More than 1,500 members** (§43a), minimum 50 delegates, no proxies, no plural votes |
| Departing member's claim on reserves | **None** (§73(3)); credit balance payable six months after membership ends (§73(2)) |
| Notice to terminate membership | Minimum three months, to a financial year end (§65) |
| Compulsory audit federation | Yes — §§53–54; federation certificate a precondition of registration (§11(2) no. 3) |
| Statutory asset lock | **None** — §§91–92 permit distribution of net assets to members |
| Investing members cap | No more than **one quarter** of the supervisory board (§8(2)) |
| Protection scheme | BVR, established **1934**; BVR-ISG the statutory deposit-guarantee arm since 2015 |
| Cooperative bank count | **771 (2021) → 735 (2022) → 697 (2023) → ~669 (2024)** — BVR and Bundesbank series. *See F.2 note 6 on the vendor "820" figure* |
| Atruvia AG | Formed 2015 (Fiducia IT AG + GAD eG); renamed 2021; agree21 platform; ~8,400 staff; ~€1.77bn group sales (2020) |

| Minimum membership | **Ten persons** for a primary society (§3(1)); six for an industrial society; five registered societies for a secondary society. *Practitioner sources commonly cite 20 for savings-and-credit societies — see F.2 note 1* |

| **Deposit boundary** | Members-only dealing is lawful; **soliciting or accepting deposits from the general public** triggers BOFIA 2020 §2 and is criminal under §2(2) — penalty not less than ten years or a fine of not less than ₦20,000,000, or both |
| **Microfinance bank capital** | **Unit Tier 2 ₦50m · Unit Tier 1 ₦200m · State ₦1bn · National ₦5bn** (2019 revision; deadlines extended by circular of 1 May 2020). *An earlier ₦20m Unit figure is superseded — see F.2 note 2* |

| Payments licences | Switching and Processing **₦2bn** · MMO **₦2bn** · PSS **₦250m** · PSSP **₦100m** · PTSP **₦100m** · **Super-Agent ₦50m** |
| Only category that may hold customer funds | **MMO** |

| IMTO fees | Application **₦10m**; annual renewal **₦10m** (by 31 January) |
| IMTO prohibitions | **Banks and fintechs barred from holding a licence**; banks may act as agents. No outbound transfers; no purchase of domestic FX; inbound must be paid in **naira**; cash payout capped at **USD 200** |
| Remittance inflows | Personal remittances **USD 20.93bn (2024)**; IMTO inflows **USD 4.73bn (2024)**, +43.5% on USD 3.30bn (2023); CBN target USD 1bn per month |
| NRBVN | Launched with NIBSS, **13 May 2025** |
| NRNOA / NRNIA accounts | Introduced 10 January 2025 |
| PAPSS circular | 28 April 2025 — eased documentation up to USD 2,000 (individuals) / USD 5,000 (companies) |

# APPENDIX F — SOURCE REGISTER AND RECONCILIATION

## F.1 Source hierarchy applied

Sources were ranked as set out in the conventions, with statute first. The principal instruments and datasets relied upon:

**Statute and regulation.** Genossenschaftsgesetz 1889 as amended (§§1, 7, 8, 8a, 11, 13, 19, 22a, 36, 43, 43a, 53–54, 65, 73, 91–92); Burgerlijk Wetboek Book 2 Title 3 (arts. 2:53–63); Quebec *Loi sur les coopératives* RLRQ c. C-67.2 and *Loi sur les coopératives de services financiers* RLRQ c. C-67.3 (arts. 84–87), Loi 141 (2018); Canada Cooperatives Act and Bank Act Part XV.1; Federal Credit Union Act 1934 (§§109, 111, 216(o)) and 12 CFR Parts 701, 702 (Subpart D), 704, 708a, 712; Capper-Volstead Act 1922; Kenya Co-operative Societies Act Cap 490, SACCO Societies Act 2008, Deposit-Taking Regulations 2010 (LN 95/2010) and Non-Deposit-Taking Regulations 2020; Nigerian Co-operative Societies Act 1993 (§§1–6, 11, 16, 17, 19, 25, 27, 29, 32, 33, 36, 37, 43, 46, 51, 52, 55, 57), Lagos State Co-operative Societies Law, CAMA 2020, BOFIA 2020 (§§2, 131), MLPPA 2022, TPPA 2022, NDPA 2023; CRR (EU) 575/2013 Articles 27–29, 49(3), 77–78, 113(7) and Delegated Regulation (EU) 241/2014 Articles 7–11, 32; IFRIC 2 and IAS 32; France Loi n° 47-1775 (1947) Articles 16, 19; Italy Civil Code Article 2514, Law 59/1992, TUB Article 37; Building Societies Act 1986.

**Regulator instruments and datasets.** CBN Revised Guidelines on International Money Transfer Services (31 January 2024) and subsequent circulars including 24 March 2026; CBN Revised MFB Guidelines (2019/2020) and circular FPR/DIR/GEN/CIR/07/054 of 1 May 2020; CBN PSB Guidelines (2018, revised 27 August 2020); CBN New Licence Categorisation for the Payments System (9 December 2020); NCUA 5300 Call Report aggregates, Quarterly Credit Union Data Summary Q4 2025 (released 6 March 2026), CUSO Registry ("CUSOs at a Glance," 31 December 2024) and legal opinions; Federal Register 71 FR 36946 (2006) and 23 December 2021 (CCULR); SASRA SACCO Supervision Annual Report 2024 (released 25 September 2025); Deutsche Bundesbank bank office statistics 2023; BVR Consolidated Financial Statements 2022; Banca d'Italia and Single Resolution Board materials on the 2017 Veneto resolutions; FHFA and Basel Committee (BIS d300) materials.

**Peer-reviewed literature.** Hansmann, *The Ownership of Enterprise* (1996) and "The Ownership of the Firm" (1988); Cook (1995) and Cook and Burress, "A Cooperative Life Cycle Framework" (2009), Cook, "A Life Cycle Explanation of Cooperative Longevity" (*Sustainability*, 2018); Ward (1958); Domar (1966); Meade (1972); Furubotn and Pejovich (1972, 1976); Olson (1965); Hart and Moore (1996, 1998); Rasmusen (1988); Stiglitz and Weiss (1981); Ghatak and Guinnane (1999); Guinnane (1994, 2001, 2003); Colvin and McLaughlin (2014); Ben-Ner (1988); Cornforth (1995); Chaddad and Cook (2004); Hesse and Čihák, IMF WP/07/02 (2007); Fonteyne, IMF WP/07/159 (2007); McKillop et al., *International Review of Financial Analysis* 71 (2020); van Rijn, Zeng and Hueth (2023); Beuthien and Klappstein on *Rücklagenauskehr*.

**Official reviews and enforcement records.** Kelly Review (30 April 2014); Myners, Report of the Independent Governance Review (7 May 2014); Treasury Select Committee, "Project Verde," HC 728 (21 October 2014); PwC forensic audit of KUSCCO (February 2025); EFCC statement of 11 March 2025; SEC Nigeria statements (October 2025).

**Used with caution as promotional.** BVR, Desjardins, Rabobank, WOCCU, ICA, CFN, CFAN and national cooperative-alliance publications; vendor case studies including IBM's Atruvia material. Their factual claims were tested against statute and regulator data, and are labelled where they could not be.

## F.2 Reconciliation of cross-stage discrepancies

The document was written in seven commissioned stages against a moving evidence base. Nine discrepancies were identified on assembly. Six are **basis, vintage or perimeter differences** and are legitimate; two are **errors or supersessions**; one is **unresolved**.

### Basis, vintage and perimeter differences — not errors

**Note 1 — Nigerian minimum membership.** Part I states **ten persons** for a primary society (§3(1) NCSA, verified against the FAOLEX text); Part VI states that a savings-and-credit society "needs only 20 members (ten for most other types)". Both are defensible. **Resolution:** §3(1) sets the *federal statutory floor* at ten for a primary society, six for an industrial society and five registered societies for a secondary society. Practitioner commentary and some state registrars require **twenty** specifically for savings-and-credit societies. **Canonical: ten is the statutory minimum; expect twenty in practice for a thrift-and-credit society; confirm with the state Registrar.** This is a statute-versus-practice difference, not a contradiction.

**Note 3 — Rabobank certificate counts.** Appendix C cites **237,961,365** certificates; the Prologue and Part II cite **313,005,461**. **Resolution:** different dates. 237,961,365 was the size of the **initial admission to Euronext Amsterdam on 27 January 2014**; 313,005,461 is the **cumulative total issued** after subsequent taps, including the 60 million issued in January 2017. Both are correct at their respective dates and Appendix E records both.

**Note 4 — Desjardins permanent shares.** Part II cites a 2012 issue targeting **C$1.2bn** that passed **C$1bn**; Appendix C cites a 2009 issue targeting **CAD 750m** at 4.25%. **Resolution:** two different issues, three years apart. Both correct. Appendix E records both with their dates.

**Note 6 — German cooperative bank count.** Part IV cites IBM's description of Atruvia as "the digitization partner of Germany's **820** cooperative banks" and "more than 800 banks" on agree21; Parts IV and Appendix C cite the **771 → 735 → 697 → ~669** series. **Resolution:** the BVR and Bundesbank series is authoritative (source-hierarchy tiers 3 and 4); IBM's figure is **vendor marketing of uncertain vintage** (tier 5) and is almost certainly stale, though it may also count entities beyond CRR credit institutions. **Canonical: the BVR/Bundesbank series.** The IBM figure is retained in Part IV as a quoted vendor claim, correctly attributed, and should not be used as a count.

**Note 7 — Desjardins predecessor federations.** Part I and Appendix E note that Desjardins' own history describes the 2001 consolidation as merging ten regional federations plus the Fédération des caisses d'économie and the Confédération, while secondary business histories cite eleven or fourteen. **Resolution: unresolved in the secondary literature and left flagged.** The consolidation date (2001) and outcome (a single Fédération) are firm.

**Note 8 — US credit-union conversion counts.** Appendix C cites both FRBSF (**33 conversions, $7.7bn, 1995–June 2007**) and NCUA (**27 conversions or mergers, 1995–2004**). **Resolution:** different windows and slightly different definitions. Both retained with their windows attached; the FRBSF figure is the canonical headline in Appendix E because its window is longer and its definition tighter.

**Note 9 — the UK survival record.** The finding that no demutualised society survived the crisis independently is stated in the Prologue, Part I and Appendix C, each time with the selection caveat. **Resolution: not a discrepancy but a repeated caveat, deliberately.** The converters self-selected as the most aggressive growers, so ownership change and risk appetite are confounded. The correct characterisation is **strong corroboration, not clean proof**, and every occurrence says so.

### Errors and supersessions

**Note 2 — Unit microfinance bank capital. GENUINE ERROR, CORRECTED.** Part II states a **₦20 million** Unit MFB minimum, citing CBN guidelines of January 2020. Part V and Part VI state **₦50 million (Unit Tier 2)** and **₦200 million (Unit Tier 1)**. **Resolution:** the ₦20m figure is the **pre-2019 threshold and is superseded.** The CBN's revision announced 7 March 2019 introduced the Tier 1 / Tier 2 split at ₦200m and ₦50m, with compliance deadlines extended by circular FPR/DIR/GEN/CIR/07/054 of 1 May 2020 to April 2021 and April 2022. **Canonical: Unit Tier 2 ₦50m; Unit Tier 1 ₦200m; State ₦1bn; National ₦5bn.** Part II's ₦20m should be read as corrected. Note that the correction does not disturb Part II's argument — the point there was that retained earnings cannot bridge to a ₦5bn National MFB, and raising the Unit floor from ₦20m to ₦50m strengthens rather than weakens it.

**Note 5 — Lagos State law citation. UNRESOLVED.** Part V cites the Lagos State Co-operative Societies Law as **Cap. C15**; Part VI cites **Cap C14, Laws of Lagos State 2015**. Neither was verified against the official gazette, and Part VI expressly flagged the citation as UNKNOWN. **Resolution: left unresolved and flagged.** Cite as "the Lagos State Co-operative Societies Law (chapter number reported variously as Cap C14 and Cap C15 — verify against the gazette)." Counsel should confirm before any filing. The substantive point — that the Lagos law mirrors the federal scheme and that the investment provision's section number could not be verified from free sources — stands regardless.

### One substantive refinement, not a discrepancy

**Note 10 — the relative weight of Cook's problems.** The Prologue describes the **horizon problem** as "the single most important concept for a founder to understand." Appendix C, having coded seventeen cases, advances a partial correction: **control** appears most frequently, and the most destructive individual cases — KUSCCO, the Co-operative Bank, the US corporate credit unions — are primarily **control** failures rather than horizon failures.

**Resolution: both stand, on their stated domains.** The horizon problem governs **mature, well-monitored cooperatives**, where it slowly starves investment and drives the demutualisation incentive. The control problem governs **young ventures in weak-monitor jurisdictions**, where it permits catastrophic fraud quickly. For this document's reader — building in Nigeria, the weakest-monitor jurisdiction of the six — **control comes first.** The Prologue's framing is not withdrawn; it is bounded. This is the most analytically interesting movement in the document and is recorded rather than harmonised away.

## F.3 Known unknowns carried forward

- Whether a Nigerian court would enforce a super-majority entrenchment clause in a cooperative bye-law against a later duly-registered amendment. Untested.
- The exact section number of the investment provision in the Lagos State Co-operative Societies Law, and the correct chapter citation.
- The current Lagos statutory minimum membership for a savings-and-credit society.
- Whether any single-shareholder percentage cap applies to a *cooperative* (as opposed to individual) shareholder in a CBN-licensed MFB. The NPF precedent (~62.6%) indicates controlling cooperative ownership is permitted in practice; the drafting of the 2020 Revised MFB Guidelines could not be verified.
- The rate and pattern of Nigerian cooperative failure. **No systematic dataset exists.** The ignorance is itself a risk factor and is treated as one.
- Whether §46 NCSA surcharge powers are used in practice. No evidence of systematic use was located.
- Contestation and turnout rates in cooperative board elections in any jurisdiction. Not systematically collected anywhere.
- The CoBank 2001 US$300m preferred issue and the Crédit Agricole 2001 new-money split — both corroborated only by secondary sources.
- Whether the NCUA's proposed 2026 rollback of 12 CFR Part 708a disclosure requirements has been finalised.
- Whether the Cooperative Bank of Nigeria will obtain a CBN licence, under what category, and whether the RH-CRRP will produce an amended Cooperative Societies Act.

## F.4 A note on evidential asymmetry

The reader should hold one structural fact about this document in view. **Evidence quality varies by an order of magnitude across the six jurisdictions.** The United States supplies a standardised quarterly return from every one of 4,287 institutions. Germany supplies audited federation accounts and a Bundesbank series. Kenya supplies a genuine annual supervision report. Nigeria supplies almost nothing: cooperative societies file nothing public, state laws are inconsistently published, and there is no register, no call report and no supervisory dataset.

The consequence is uncomfortable and must be stated plainly. **Parts I to IV rest on statute, regulator data and audited accounts. Part V rests on statute, circulars and secondary legal commentary. Part VI rests on inference from all of it.** The Part the reader most needs is the Part with the weakest evidence base — and the near-total absence of documented Nigerian cooperative failures should be read as **ignorance, not safety.** The base rate of failure in Nigeria is almost certainly higher than the visible record suggests, precisely because the monitoring that would record it does not exist.
